About this tracker

This tool keeps an eye on Microsoft's Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS), AKS Application Network, Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes, AKS Arc, Kubernetes Fleet Manager, Azure Linux, Azure Container Registry (ACR), and Azure Application Gateway for Containers (AGC) documentation. AKS everywhere! It also shows the last 5 AKS and App Net release notes.

It automatically scans for changes, then uses AI to summarize and highlight updates that are most likely to matter — such as new features, deprecations, and significant content revisions.

Minor edits (like typos, formatting tweaks, and other low-impact changes) are usually filtered out. Because the process is automated, some updates may be missed or summaries may not capture every nuance.

For complete accuracy, you can always follow the provided links to the original Microsoft documentation.

With this tracker, you can:

  • Quickly scan meaningful AKS, App Net, Arc K8s, AKS Arc, Azure Linux, ACR, AGC, and Fleet documentation changes from the past 7 days
  • Stay up to date with the latest AKS and App Net release notes without digging through every doc page
  • Search and browse CVE security data across AKS releases and VHD node images, powered by the AKS Vulnerability Data API (Public Preview)

Get the Latest AKS Docs - Every Week, in Your Inbox

AKS Releases

Latest 5 AKS and App Net release notes with AI-generated summaries, breaking changes, and Good to Know information.

updated: 28/10/2026, 21:10:59

Release 2026-10-25

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-10-27

The release on 2026-10-25 introduces automatic upgrades for unsupported Kubernetes versions and updates to node pools.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AKS will automatically upgrade unsupported clusters after a grace period.
  • Default OS SKU for node pools changes to Ubuntu 26.04 with Kubernetes 1.38.
🔑

Key Features

  • Kubernetes 1.37 is now generally available as Long-Term Support.
  • Node Auto Provisioning (NAP) enhancements include new CRDs and features.
💡

Good to Know

  • Review upgrade channels to prepare for automated upgrades.
  • Community support for Kubernetes 1.34 ends by November 2026.

Release Notes 2026-09-25

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-10-01

The 2026-09-25 release introduces significant updates and deprecations. Key features include the general availability of identity bindings and preview support for Ubuntu 26.04.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • Windows Server 2022 is not supported in Kubernetes version 1.37 and above.
  • Flatcar Container Linux node images are removed, affecting scaling operations.
  • The annotation service.beta.kubernetes.io/azure-pip-tags no longer overrides public IP tags.
🔑

Key Features

  • Identity bindings are now generally available.
  • Ubuntu 26.04 is available in preview.
  • Windows Server OS SKU migration to 2025 is in preview.
💡

Good to Know

  • AKS will auto-migrate deprecated Availability Sets clusters after September 30, 2026.
  • AKS release order has been updated for Central US and East US.
  • Check AKS Release Tracker for detailed release timing.

Release 2026-09-04

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-09-09

This release introduces Kubernetes 1.37 and autoscaling for VM node pools. It also announces the retirement of Azure Linux with OS Guard.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AKS will remove Azure Linux with OS Guard node images on March 10, 2027.
  • AKS no longer produces VHDs for Windows Server 2019 and version 23H2.
🔑

Key Features

  • Autoscaling for Virtual Machines node pools is now generally available.
  • Kubernetes patch versions 1.36.3, 1.35.7, and 1.34.10 are available.
  • On-Demand Monitor for node health checks is in preview.
💡

Good to Know

  • LocalDNS is enabled automatically for compatible cluster networking.
  • AKS rejects updates that remove IPv6 from dual-stack clusters.
  • Managed namespace creation rejects names starting with reserved prefixes.

AppNet 2026-08-19

App Net
View Release
📅 2026-08-31

AppNet is now in public preview with several updates and bug fixes.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AppNet 1.0, 1.1, and 1.2 versions retired.
  • Moving AppNet resources across resource groups is now blocked.
🔑

Key Features

  • AppNet available in more Azure regions.
  • AppNet 1.5 updated to Istio 1.30.3.
💡

Good to Know

  • Cluster prerequisites validated at member-join time.
  • Fixed issues with member updates and version upgrades.

Release 2026-08-07

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-08-11

This release introduces several enhancements including Node Auto Provisioning and automatic availability zone placement.

🔑

Key Features

  • Node Auto Provisioning now supports restricted public network access
  • Automatic availability zone placement is enabled globally
  • Control-plane only upgrades to AKS Long Term Support are allowed
  • Node pool rollback feature is now generally available
  • Encryption in Transit for Azure File CSI driver is now available

Meaningful updates to AKS, App Net, Arc K8s, AKS Arc, Azure Linux, ACR, AGC, and Fleet docs from the last 7 days.

              ACR - Container Registry Geo Replication

              General ✨ Update Detected: 2026-10-28 00:16 UTC
              Summary

              The documentation now clarifies that when using static private IP allocation for Azure Container Registry, adding geo-replicas or enabling regional endpoints can fail if the required member-to-IP mappings are not configured. Users are advised to create the private endpoint only after all geo-replicas are established to ensure proper IP configuration, and to use dynamic IP allocation if they plan to add geo-replicas or enable regional endpoints later, as this will automate the necessary member mappings.

If access was recently granted, please refresh your credentials."+    }+  }+```++### Cause++This error happens when the identity that the Azure Files CSI driver uses doesn't have enough Azure role-based access control (Azure RBAC) permissions to perform file share operations on the target storage account.++Common causes include the following:++- The AKS cluster identity is missing Azure RBAC role assignments.+- Role assignments are configured at an incorrect scope.+- A custom role doesn't include Azure Files management permissions.+- Recent permission changes didn't propagate through Azure RBAC yet.+- You granted the role to the wrong identity. For dynamic provisioning, the identity in the error is the AKS cluster (control plane) identity, not the kubelet identity.++### Troubleshooting++The following steps can help you troubleshoot the `AuthorizationFailed` error during Azure Files share provisioning.++#### Step 1: Identify the principal reported in the error++Review the PVC events and note the `<CLIENT_ID>` and `<OBJECT_ID>` values. These values identify the principal that's trying to access the Azure file share.++#### Step 2: Determine which AKS identity you're using++Check the AKS cluster configuration to identify whether the cluster uses a system-assigned managed identity, a user-assigned managed identity, or a service principal.++Retrieve the cluster identity information. Run the following command.++```azurecli+az aks show \+  --resource-group <AKS_RESOURCE_GROUP> \+  --name <AKS_CLUSTER_NAME> \+  --query identity+```++For clusters that use managed identities, verify the kubelet identity. Run the following command.++```azurecli+az aks show \+  --resource-group <AKS_RESOURCE_GROUP> \+  --name <AKS_CLUSTER_NAME> \+  --query identityProfile+```++Confirm that the client ID reported in the provisioning error matches the AKS cluster identity (`identity`) that you're using. For file share provisioning, the error usually reports the cluster (control plane) identity, not the kubelet identity (`identityProfile`)++#### Step 3: Verify role assignments++Check whether the identified principal has role assignments on the target storage account. Run the following command.++```azurecli+az role assignment list \+  --assignee <CLIENT_ID> \+  --scope /subscriptions/<SUBSCRIPTION_ID>/resourceGroups/<RESOURCE_GROUP>/providers/Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/<STORAGE_ACCOUNT> \+  --include-inherited+```++Review the assigned roles and scopes. The `--include-inherited` parameter is required to list role assignments that are inherited from the resource group or subscription scope. Without it, the command doesn't return those assignments.++#### Step 4: Validate required permissions++The Azure Files CSI driver must be able to perform Azure Files management operations through Azure Resource Manager. At a minimum, the assigned role must allow the following operations.++```text+Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/fileServices/shares/read+Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/fileServices/shares/write+Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/fileServices/shares/delete+```++For most scenarios, assigning the built-in **Contributor** role at the storage account scope is sufficient.++#### Step 5: Verify the role assignment scope++Make sure that the role assignment applies to a scope that includes the target storage account:++- Storage account (recommended)+- Resource group+- Subscription++A role assignment on another resource or scope doesn't grant access to the storage account that the StorageClass references.++#### Step 6: Allow time for Azure RBAC propagation++If you recently granted or changed permissions, wait for Azure RBAC propagation, and then retry the operation. Role assignment changes can take several minutes to take effect. Follow these steps:++1. Re-create the PVC or retry provisioning.+2. Monitor the PVC events.+3. Verify that the Azure file share is created.+4. Confirm that the PVC transitions to the `Bound` state.++### Solution++Grant the required Azure RBAC permissions to the identity that the Azure Files CSI driver uses on the target storage account. After the role assignment propagates, retry provisioning and verify that the Azure file share can be accessed.++Note++An `AuthorizationFailed` error indicates an Azure Resource Manager (ARM) authorization issue, not a network connectivity or mounting issue. Focus your troubleshooting on identity configuration, Azure RBAC permissions, and role assignment scope.+ ## References  If you experience other mount errors, see [Troubleshoot Azure Files problems in Linux](/en-us/azure/storage/files/storage-troubleshoot-linux-file-connection-problems).

By configuring limits on pods, you can avoid memory pressure on the node. Make sure that the aggregate memory requests for pods that are running don't exceed the node's allocatable memory. Setting limits much higher than requests, or not setting limits, can increase the risk of memory overcommitment at runtime. The Kubernetes scheduler places pods based on resource requests, not actual usage. Additionally, while the kubelet is evicting pods, it prioritizes pods in which the memory usage exceeds their defined requests. Use HPA when memory usage increases proportionally with load and distributing requests across additional replicas reduces per-pod consumption.                     AKS - Overview

                    Security ✨ Update Detected: 2026-10-23 20:37 UTC
                    Summary

                    The documentation now includes a new section detailing the security bulletin AKS-2026-0006, which addresses OpenSSL end-of-life findings in AKS-managed add-on images. Users are informed that while some vulnerability scanners may report OpenSSL 3.3 as end-of-life, Azure Linux continues to provide security fixes through backports, and they should verify package versions using the image's SBOM or RPM metadata rather than relying solely on the upstream version. Additionally, guidance is provided on how to handle EOL warnings and CVEs, emphasizing the importance of using distribution-aware matching for accurate vulnerability assessments.

                      @@ -12,6 +12,81 @@ These updates cover security information related to the following AKS components - Azure Kubernetes Service Node Image (AKS Node Image) - Azure Kubernetes Service add-ons (AKS add-ons) +## AKS-2026-0006 OpenSSL end-of-life scanner findings in AKS-managed add-on images++**Published date**: October 21, 2026++### Description++Some vulnerability scanners report OpenSSL 3.3 as end of life (EOL) in AKS-managed add-on container images. This article explains why an upstream EOL warning alone doesn't establish that the Azure Linux package lacks security maintenance.++OpenSSL 3.3 reached [upstream EOL on April 9, 2026](https://openssl-library.org/roadmap/index.html). Azure Linux 3.0 continues to apply security fixes to its OpenSSL package through backports. A backport applies a fix without changing the upstream version number.++The [Azure Linux OpenSSL package changelog](https://github.com/microsoft/azurelinux/blob/59dd31f473b6eca8d05410731653177444d6a416/SPECS/openssl/openssl.spec#L387-L401) documents security fixes after that date:++| Azure Linux package revision | Changelog date | Example security fix |+| --- | --- | --- |+| `3.3.7-4.azl3` | July 16, 2026 | [CVE-2026-45447 patch](https://github.com/microsoft/azurelinux/blob/59dd31f473b6eca8d05410731653177444d6a416/SPECS/openssl/CVE-2026-45447.patch) |+| `3.3.7-6.azl3` | August 26, 2026 | [CVE-2026-54874 patch](https://github.com/microsoft/azurelinux/blob/59dd31f473b6eca8d05410731653177444d6a416/SPECS/openssl/CVE-2026-54874.patch) |++These examples show backporting, not a minimum package version or a claim that every image contains every fix.++Important++This article doesn't classify all OpenSSL findings as false positives. An upstream EOL warning and a missing security patch are different findings. Assess each reported CVE against the exact Azure Linux package revision.++### Why scanners report these findings++The `openssl version` command reports the upstream version, such as `3.3.7`, without the Azure Linux package revision, such as `-4.azl3`. The image's RPM package metadata and software bill of materials (SBOM) contain the complete package version.++A scanner that evaluates only the upstream version can miss Microsoft's backported fixes. The upstream EOL date is correct, but an inference that Microsoft no longer services the Azure Linux package is incorrect.++Some scanners also report CVEs without accounting for distribution-specific fixes. The [Azure Linux 3.0 vulnerability feed](https://github.com/microsoft/AzureLinuxVulnerabilityData/blob/main/azurelinux-3.0-oval.xml) provides Open Vulnerability and Assessment Language (OVAL) definitions for evaluating CVEs against package revisions. Distribution-aware CVE matching doesn't remove an EOL-only warning that has no associated CVE.++The container's base operating system is separate from the node's operating system. An add-on running on an Ubuntu node can use an Azure Linux base image. A node image upgrade doesn't change the packages inside that container image.++### Evidence from a published image++For the `linux/amd64` image `mcr.microsoft.com/oss/v2/kubernetes/coredns:v1.14.3-20`, the [digest-pinned SPDX SBOM](https://mcr.microsoft.com/v2/oss/v2/kubernetes/coredns/blobs/sha256:6f5dde31f4349cfbf4f532dc9b8ab090409047e6beb74c6cf8a5542801d65481) records:++| Package | Complete package version |+| --- | --- |+| `azurelinux-release` | `3.0-49.azl3` |+| `openssl` | `3.3.7-4.azl3` |+| `openssl-libs` | `3.3.7-4.azl3` |++The public [image manifest](https://mcr.microsoft.com/v2/oss/v2/kubernetes/coredns/manifests/sha256:6903c44b9016c274048ffc4b1b3691fb6ac6ccd82f2d2e19032142f80cea9339) and [attestation manifest](https://mcr.microsoft.com/v2/oss/v2/kubernetes/coredns/manifests/sha256:1f52d1dfa4fab42f5a7ab597ebb38782a85f7977193a7e6a15fcb5b53f307d2e) link that image to this SBOM. These links use immutable digests rather than a moving tag.++This historical image illustrates package identification, not a recommendation to deploy it or a statement that it has no vulnerabilities. Check your deployed digest separately.++### References++- [OpenSSL release roadmap and upstream support dates](https://openssl-library.org/roadmap/index.html).+- [Azure Linux OpenSSL package and backport changelog](https://github.com/microsoft/azurelinux/blob/59dd31f473b6eca8d05410731653177444d6a416/SPECS/openssl/openssl.spec#L387-L401).+- [Azure Linux 3.0 vulnerability data and OVAL definitions](https://github.com/microsoft/AzureLinuxVulnerabilityData/blob/main/azurelinux-3.0-oval.xml).+- [Vulnerability management for AKS](../concepts-vulnerability-management).++### Affected components++# [AKS add-ons](#tab/aks-addons)+**Affected versions**++- AKS-managed add-on images based on Azure Linux 3.0 that contain OpenSSL 3.3 packages can trigger these findings.+- Examples include CoreDNS, metrics-server, and Azure Disk and Azure Files CSI components. The finding depends on the exact image and the scanner's detection rules, not a single Kubernetes version.+- This bulletin doesn't cover OpenSSL packages in node images or customer-managed containers.++**Resolutions**++No manual OpenSSL replacement is needed for a verified EOL-only finding in the scope of this bulletin. AKS manages updates to its add-on images. A newer add-on image can contain security fixes and still report the same upstream OpenSSL version.++- **Verify the package.** Record the image repository, tag, and digest from the scanner report. Confirm Azure Linux 3.0 and the complete `openssl` or `openssl-libs` package revision from that image's SBOM or RPM metadata. Don't use `openssl version` alone to assess the package's patch status.+- **Separate EOL warnings from CVEs.** For a specific CVE, compare the installed package revision with Azure Linux's published fix information. Ask your scanner vendor to use distribution-aware matching and current vulnerability data.+- **Handle verified EOL-only findings through your security policy.** Consider a time-bound exception for the exact image digest and EOL finding. Attach the package version, the published backport evidence, and a review date. Don't extend the exception to unresolved CVEs.+- **Keep managed components intact.** Don't replace or modify AKS-managed add-on images to change the reported OpenSSL version. Continue normal cluster and node image updates for other security fixes.+- **Escalate findings outside this scope.** Contact Azure support if your report identifies a missing security patch or differs from this bulletin's scope. Include the image digest, full package version, finding identifier, and scanner output.++---+ ## AKS-2026-0005 Increased Ubuntu 22.04 Linux kernel CVE findings from Canonical reclassifications  **Published Date**: July 7, 2026

Learn how to identify the nodes and containers that consume excessive CPU and apply best practices to restore performance and reliability.+ High CPU usage is a symptom of one or more applications or processes that require so much CPU time that the performance or usability of the machine is impacted. High CPU usage can occur in many ways, but user configuration mostly causes it.  When a node in an [Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS)](/en-us/azure/aks/intro-kubernetes) cluster experiences high CPU usage, the applications running on it can experience degradation in performance and reliability. Applications or processes also become unstable, which might lead to problems beyond slow responses. -This article helps you identify the nodes and containers that consume high CPU and provides best practices to resolve high CPU usage.- ## Symptoms -The following table outlines the common symptoms of high CPU usage:+The following table outlines the common symptoms of high CPU usage.  | Symptom | Description | | --- | --- | | CPU starvation | CPU-intensive applications slow down other applications on the same node. |-| Slow state changes | Pods may take longer to get ready. |+| Slow state changes | Pods might take longer to get ready. | | NotReady node state | Severe CPU contention can starve the kubelet of CPU time and prevent it from sending timely status updates to the API server. You can choose between two modes: Shared and ServiceNodePort. The Shared mode uses a single health probe for all external traffic policy cluster services that use the same load balancer. In contrast, the ServiceNodePort mode uses a separate health probe for each service.                                                                                                   @@ -1,8 +1,10 @@-# Improve container image pull performance in Azure Kubernetes Service - Azure | Microsoft Learn+# Improve container image pull performance in AKS - Azure | Microsoft Learn  ## Summary -Container image pull performance can be affected by the image size and layer count, node CPU and disk performance, network connectivity, registry location and service tier, and the number of concurrent pulls. For large supported images, [Artifact Streaming](/en-us/azure/aks/artifact-streaming) can reduce pod startup time.                                                                                                                       AKS - Use Capacity Reservation Groups

                                                                                                                      Security ✨ Update Detected: 2026-10-27 18:10 UTC
                                                                                                                      Summary

                                                                                                                      The documentation now includes instructions for associating an existing capacity reservation group (CRG) with both new clusters and new system node pools in Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS). Users can create a cluster with a CRG by using the `az aks create` command with the `--assign-identity` and `--crg-id` flags, and they can also add a CRG to a new system node pool using the `az aks nodepool add` command with the `--mode System` and `--crg-id` flags. Additionally, guidance has been clarified for managing non-zonal system node pools without needing to scale them to zero.
